## Page 1


REAL-VARIABLE THEORY OF FUNCTION SPACES WITH
OPERATOR-VALUED Ap WEIGHTS IN BANACH SPACES
TUOMAS P. HYT ¨ONEN, YINQIN LI, DACHUN YANG, AND WEN YUAN
Abstract. While the theory of matrix-weighted function spaces is well established, the majority
of previous results in the infinite-dimensional operator-valued setting deal with “no go” theorems,
showing the impossibility of some prospective generalizations. However, we show that a com-
plete real-variable theory of Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces with operator-valued Muckenhoupt
Ap weights can still be developed, once correctly formulated. This covers operator-weighted ex-
tensions of results like the φ-transform characterization in terms of discrete sequence spaces, the
boundedness of almost diagonal operators, and applications to the T(1) theorem and trace/extension
theorems. A key tool is a version of the reverse H¨older inequality, which is weak enough to follow
from the operator-valued Ap condition (unlike a variant that had to be imposed as an additional as-
sumption in some previous works), yet strong enough to be used much like its classical counterpart.
In contrast to the established scalar and matrix-weighted theories, our approach cannot build on
operator-weighted Lp results, as these fail in a very definite way. We also strengthen the existing
“no go” statements in Hilbert spaces, showing (among other counterexamples) that every infinite-
dimensional Banach space has an operator-valued Ap weight V for which the Hilbert transform is
unbounded on Lp(V). This is nontrivial, since the lack of Hilbert space structure also complicates
the construction of Ap weights. Building on results from Banach space theory, we achieve this
unboundedness by combining two distinct methods in two different classes of spaces (so-called
K-convex ones and those that are not UMD), whose union covers all Banach spaces.

1. Introduction
Since its discovery in the 1970’s, Muckenhoupt’s theory of Ap weights has become one of the
central topics in harmonic analysis with diverse applications. Originally dealing with scalar-valued
weight functions, the extension of this theory to matrix Ap weights is also well developed since
the 1990’s.
The theme of this paper is operator-valued Ap weights, a further extension of the matrix Ap
theory to possibly infinite-dimensional target spaces. Much less is known about this area, and
much of what is known concerns “no go” theorems pointing to the failure of natural extensions of
familiar results from finite dimensions, even in the supposedly simplest case of p = 2 and a Hilbert
space target, not to mention other values of p and more general Banach spaces, where relatively
little has been said so far.
Nevertheless, at least a definition of Ap weights is readily available in great generality. While
much of the recent literature on matrix Ap weights has been based on an equivalent (in finite
dimensions) condition given by Roudenko [59, Lemma 1.3], the original definition by Nazarov–
Treil [55, p. 21] and Volberg [66, p. 450], stated in terms of dual norms, makes perfect sense
for an arbitrary Banach space X. In a way, this generality even clarifies the picture, since one
can no longer identify X with its dual X∗, making it necessary to distinguish between norms on
the original space and on the dual space. Some aspects of this general framework were already
developed by Lauzon [46], but many of his results are only proved under additional postulates
concerning versions of the reverse H¨older inequality that are not implied by the Ap condition
in general; for p = 2 and Hilbert targets, such theory under reverse H¨older postulates was first
developed by Pott [58].
The aim of this article is to show that the natural Ap condition for operator-valued weights,
acting on a general Banach space,
(i) retains several key features of the classical Ap condition;
(ii) is necessary but insufficient for a weighted theory in Lp;


## Page 3


OPERATOR-VALUED Ap WEIGHTS IN BANACH SPACES
3
(iii) is nevertheless sufficient for developing substantial parts of the theory of function spaces of
Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin type.
We will elaborate on each of these points shortly, but let us first provide some more context.
In both the scalar-weighted and the matrix-weighted cases, the resolution of the basic problems
in Lp (like the boundedness of the maximal operator and the Hilbert transform) came first, and
served as stepping stones for the weighted theory of other function spaces. For scalar weights, the
said Lp results were obtained in [52] and [38], respectively, and for matrix weights, in [55, 65, 66]
and [17, 35], in the reverse order. The scalar-weighted theory of Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin
spaces seems to have started with [15], the matrix-weighted theory of Besov spaces with [59], and
a systematic theory of matrix-weighted Triebel–Lizorkin spaces with [28].
Against this background, the fact that we obtain a reasonable theory of Besov and Triebel–
Lizorkin spaces with operator weights, even in the lack of a corresponding Lp theory, might seem
surprising. However, a natural point of analogy is found in the (unweighted) theory of singular
integrals of Banach space valued functions. The problem of the Lp boundedness of basic singular
operators is understood in this situation, and known to be characterised by the UMD property of
the target space X; this is a celebrated result of Burkholder [16] and Bourgain [8]; see also [39,
Theorem 5.1.1] for a detailed treatment. However, the boundedness on (unweighted) Besov and
Triebel–Lizorkin spaces is available much more generally, without any restrictions on X (see [41,
Chapter 14]). What we obtain for operator-weighted spaces is essentially the same, with the only
difference that we do not have the characterisation of the weights for the Lp boundedness. But
even in the unweighted theory, the known characterisation of spaces by the UMD property does
not play any role in the development of the theory of Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces with
values in arbitrary spaces.
The rest of this Introduction is divided into three sections, where we elaborate on each of the
points (i), (ii), and (iii) above, indicating our main results and providing some further background.
The rest of the paper is then divided into four parts, where we develop the theory on each of
these points in full detail. The content and the order of both Sections 1.1, 1.2, and 1.3 of this
Introduction, and Parts 1, 2, and 3 of the rest of the paper, is the same as that of points (i), (ii), and
(iii) above. The final Part 4 continues further with point (iii) within a new axiomatic framework.
1.1. Key properties of Ap weights. Concerning point (i) above, some key lemmas for scalar and
matrix weights that we are able to extend to our general setting are
(a) reverse H¨older inequalities (Proposition 5.1);
(b) quantitative doubling properties (Lemma 6.7).
The role of reverse H¨older inequalities for both scalar and matrix weights is well established
since [18] and [17, 35], respectively. For operator-valued weights, as already mentioned, Pott [58]
(for p = 2 and Hilbert targets) and Lauzon [46] (more generally) showed that many results can
still be extended, provided that versions of reverse H¨older inequalities are assumed, but these are
genuine additional assumptions on the weights, no longer consequences of the Ap condition as
they were in finite dimensions.
We wish to make the claim that, in the operator-weighted setting, the notion of a reverse H¨older
inequality should be (only slightly) revised from the matrix-valued version of [17, 35]. This re-
vised formulation has the benefit of being implied by the operator-valued Ap condition (as we
show in Proposition 5.1) while still strong enough to serve the role of the classical reverse H¨older
inequality in several applications, notably in the development of the theory of Besov and Triebel–
Lizorkin spaces with operator weights further below.
The role of various doubling properties is also well established in analysis. In point (b) above,
we have especially in mind types of estimates that were introduced into the analysis of matrix-
weighted Besov spaces by Roudenko [60, Definition 1.3], and that have played a significant role
in related developments ever since; cf. [28, Definition 2.1] and [10, Section 2.1]. In the matrix-
weighted theory, these estimates take the form
∥AQA−1

T. P. HYT ¨ONEN, Y. LI, D. YANG, AND W. YUAN
matrix inducing an equivalent Euclidean norm for a given norm on Rm or Cm; it is produced by
the John ellipsoid theorem [43], a strictly finite-dimensional tool.
While we have no analogue for reducing operators in infinite-dimensional study, we can still
find useful substitutes for bounds of the form (1.1) with the left-hand side replaced by the ratio
of the operator-weight-induced norms over the cubes Q and R. Indeed, such a result is proved in
Lemma 6.7, and then successfully applied in the theory of operator-weighted Besov and Triebel–
Lizorkin spaces in Part 3 of the paper.
1.2. Lp results. Concerning operator-weighted Lp spaces, we are going to show that the operator-
valued Ap condition remains
(a) necessary and sufficient for the weighted Lp boundedness of basic averaging operators (Corol-
lary 8.3; previously proved for p = 2 and Hilbert targets by Aleman–Constantin [1, Lemma
2.2]);
(b) sufficient for the weighted Lp boundedness of certain sparse operators (Corollary 8.4; previ-
ously proved for p = 2 and Hilbert targets by Limani–Pott [49, Theorem 2.1]); but
(c) insufficient for the weighted Lp boundedness of the Hilbert transform H (Theorem 10.1; pre-
viously proved for p = 2 and Hilbert targets by Gillespie–Pott–Treil–Volberg [32, Theorem
1.3], with a sharper version in [34, Theorem 1.1]);
(d) as a tool for (c) but with independent interest, we show that certain operator-valued bounded
mean oscillation (BMO) conditions are similarly insufficient for the boundedness of the com-
mutator [B, H] on the unweighted Lp(R; X) (Theorem 9.1; previously proved for p = 2 and
Hilbert targets by Nazarov–Pisier–Treil–Volberg [54, Theorem 1.2] and Gillespie–Pott–Treil–
Volberg [32, Theorem 1.2], [34, Theorem 1.3]; see also [33] for related results on martingale
transforms.).
Related to point (d), we should also mention the classical works of Sarason [62] and Page [56]
on the factorisation of Hardy spaces of operator-valued functions and the boundedness of Hankel
operators with operator-valued symbols, both of which are known to be essentially equivalent to
the boundedness of the commutators [B, H] with operator-valued B. In contrast to the negative
results of point (d), [62] and [56] achieve characterisations in infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces,
but these are expressed in terms of quotient norms whose relation to the real-variable theory that
we develop remains unclear.
The sparse operators appearing in (b) have become instrumental in the scalar-weighted theory
since the work of Lerner [47], and also in the matrix-weighted theory since the work of Nazarov–
Petermichl–Treil–Volberg [53], thanks to the fact they dominate classical operators in a way that is
compatible with both scalar and matrix weighted estimates. In contrast to this, the positive result
about the operator-weighted boundedness of sparse operators in (b), together with the negative
result for the Hilbert transform in (c), shows that Calder´on-Zygmund operators are no longer
dominated by sparse operators in the infinite-dimensional setting, making sparse operators less
relevant objects in this theory than in finite dimensions. For p = 2 and Hilbert targets, this was
already pointed out by Limani–Pott [49].
In some sense, points (c) and (d) are perhaps expected; after all, if things go wrong even in
a Hilbert space, as already shown in [32, 34, 54], how could they possibly behave better in more
complicated situations. However, the “good” properties of a Hilbert space also ensure the existence
of a plenty of operators (such as bounded projections onto any closed subspace, certainly not
available in a general Banach space), therefore also a plenty of operator-valued Ap weights (resp.
BMO functions), making a condition over all Ap weights (resp. BMO functions) more difficult to
be satisfied than in a setting with few Ap weights (resp. BMO functions) only. In another extreme,
there are Banach spaces with “very few operators”, in the sense that every bounded linear operator
has the form λ · I + K of a scalar-multiple of the identity plus a compact operator K; the first such
example is a celebrated construction of Argyros and Haydon [3]. In a space with this scalar-plus-
compact property, one might imagine that operator Ap weights (resp. BMO functions) are “not
far” from matrix Ap weights (resp. BMO functions), making it plausible that the positive results
of the finite-dimensional theory might still be pushed to hold. This suggests that achieving the
negative results in (c) and (d) for any given Banach space X requires some effort.

OPERATOR-VALUED Ap WEIGHTS IN BANACH SPACES
5
Our constructions of the counterexamples in both (c) and (d) split into two cases using different
properties of the Banach space under consideration. The first case is when X is not a UMD space,
in which case the Hilbert transform is unbounded on the unweighted Lp(R; X). The other case is
when X is K-convex, in which case the counterexamples are obtained by carefully embedding a
sequence of the finite dimensional Hilbertian counterexamples of [54] into X in a quantitatively
controlled manner, using an elaboration of Dvoretzky’s theorem in K-convex spaces due to Figiel
and Tomczak-Jaegermann [23] as a key tool. Since every UMD space is K-convex, but not con-
versely, these two cases cover all Banach spaces, even with some overlap.

Once we have laid down these foundations of the theory of operator-weighted Besov and
Triebel–Lizorkin spaces in Part 3, it turns out that extensions of many more results from the
matrix-weighted versions of these spaces can be developed in an axiomatic framework, taking
only a few of the key properties established in Part 3 as postulates of an abstract class of dis-
cretizable spaces that we study in Part 4. The results obtained in this abstract setting include
molecular and wavelet characterizations of these spaces and their applications to the T(1) theorem
for Calder´on–Zygmund operators. We also apply these results to the boundedness of trace and
extension operators in operator-weighted Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces. The results of Part
4 extend several earlier results in the matrix-weighted setting from [26, 27, 28, 59].
In the classical theory, Triebel–Lizorkin spaces arise as an extension of the scale of Lp(Rn) =
˙F0
p,2(Rn) for p ∈(1, ∞), and this relation persists even for matrix-Ap-weighted versions, as shown
in [28, Section 4]. In view of (c) and (d) above, while recalling (c) of Section 1.2, the operator-
weighted Triebel–Lizorkin spaces should rather be seen as a substitute for Lp(V), being relatively
close to Lp(V) according to (d), yet sufficiently different so that good properties that fail in Lp(V)
remain true in ˙Fs
p,q(ρ). This phenomenon is already present in the unweighted but Banach space
-valued versions of these spaces (cf. [41, p. 294]); and we extend it to the general operator Ap
weighted setting.
Matrix-weighted Besov spaces were extensively studied in [26, 27, 59, 60] and a systematic
study of matrix-weighted Triebel–Lizorkin spaces was started in [28]. This has been followed by

T. P. HYT ¨ONEN, Y. LI, D. YANG, AND W. YUAN
numerous extensions by many authors [9, 10, 11, 12, 13, 14, 4, 5, 48, 67]. However, all these
works deal with finite-dimensional spaces, where operators are just finite matrices.
Despite the various “no go” theorems around the Hilbert transform in operator-weighted Lp
spaces mentioned above, an important indication for us, that positive results could still be achieved
in other spaces, was a work of Aleman and Constantin [1], where they characterised the bound-
edness of the Bergman projection on operator-weighted L2 spaces in terms of an operator-valued
B´ekoll´e–Bonami B2 condition—a well-known relative of the Muckenhoupt A2 condition in the
Bergman context. While their precise setting is different, it also has important similarities with the
theory of Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces. Notably, the arguments of [1] make critical use of
the mean value property of analytic functions, which bears some similarity with versions of the
Calder´on reproducing formula commonly applied in the context of Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin
spaces; indeed, the components of the Littlewood–Paley decomposition are analytic functions!
Building on this analogy, we are able to extend a lot of the recent matrix-weighted theory of Besov
and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces to the operator-weighted context, achieving the types of results listed
above.
While the settings are not directly comparable, our results go arguably beyond mere analogues
of those of [1] in the following sense: In [1], only L2 spaces of Hilbert space-valued functions and
the corresponding B2 weights are considered, while we treat the full scale of Besov and Triebel–
Lizorkin spaces ˙As
p,q of Banach space-valued distributions and the corresponding Muckenhoupt
classes Ap for all p ∈(0, ∞). (Recall that, already in the matrix-weighted setting, the single scalar-
valued A1 condition splits into a scale of weight classes Ap with p ∈(0, 1], first introduced by
Frazier and Roudenko [26].) This, in turn, leads to the following qualitative difference: In the
Hilbertian L2 case, the reducing operator can be computed explicitly by

Part 1. Basic properties of operator weights
In this part, we develop the relevant background for operator-valued weights to be used in the
study of operator-weighted Lp spaces in Part 2 and operator-weighted Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin
spaces in Part 3.

Let us start with a brief background for the setting that we are going to adopt.

If f : Rn →Cm and we would like to weigh its value by a matrix-valued weight, only the

see that it is de facto V, and not W, that is the primary object of study, and the repeated appearance
of p-th roots in the formulas is mostly a notational nuisance only caused by the insistence of
writing expression in terms of W instead of the more natural V. This point becomes even more
prominent when we pass to a general Banach space X.
Let us then motivate a notion of L (X)-valued weights V and weighted spaces Lp(V) of X-valued
functions, modelled after the right-hand side of (3.1). It seems reasonable to require that
(1) Lp(V) ⊆L0(X);
(2) Lp(V) ⊇{ f ∈L0(X) simple and boundedly supported};
(3) given f ∈L0(X), we have f ∈Lp(V) if and only if V f ∈Lp(X);
(4) given g ∈Lp(X), we have V−1g ∈Lp(V).
Let Q be a cube and e ∈X. Since 1Qe is simple and boundedly supported, it follows that 1Qe ∈
Lp(V) by (2), hence 1QVe ⊆Lp(X) by (3). Being true for all cubes Q and e ∈X, this shows that

L0(X) by (1). Being true for all cubes Q and e ∈X, this shows that V−1 ∈L0
so(L (X)).

The following result clarifies the role of condition (iii) in Definition 3.1:
Proposition 3.2. Let X be a Banach space and V : Rn →L (X) be a function that satisfies (i) and
(ii) of Definition 3.1.
(i) If X is separable, then V also satisfies (iii) of Definition 3.1.
(ii) Without separability, the previous conclusion fails, in general.
Proof. (i): For a separable Banach space X, we recall (see [39, Corollary 1.1.10]) that a function
f : Rn →X is strongly measurable (a pointwise limit of simple functions) if and only if it is
measurable (i.e., the inverse image f −1(B) is a measurable subset of Rn for every B in Bor(X),
the Borel σ-algebra generated by the open subsets of X). The argument that follows is more
conveniently expressed via the latter notion of inverse images.
Let X and V be as in the assumptions. We consider the map

This is a so-called Carath´eodory function [2, Definition 14.74]:

V ∈L0
so(Rn; X), combined with the above remark about measurability and strong measura-
bility.
(ii) Φ(x, ·) = V(x) : X →X is continuous. This is clear from V(x) ∈L (X).
By [2, Lemma 14.75], Carath´eodory functions are jointly measurable, i.e., for every closed F ⊆X,
the pre-image Φ−1(F) satisfies

where the right-hand side is the product σ-algebra. In particular, we can take F = {u} with u ∈X.

The following result, collecting some basic facts about the Lp(V) spaces just introduced, is

Proposition 3.7. Let X be a Banach space, p ∈(0, ∞], and V : Rn →L (X) be a p-weight. Then
(i) Lp(V) is isometrically isomorphic to Lp(Rn; X), where the isomorphisms are given by point-
wise multiplication by V : Lp(V) →Lp(Rn; X) and its pointwise inverse V−1 : Lp(Rn; X) →
Lp(V).
(ii) Lp(V) is a Banach space if p ≥1, or a quasi-Banach space if p < 1.
(iii) { f ∈L0(X) simple, boundedly supported} ⊆Lp(V); it is dense if p < ∞.

5. Reverse H¨older properties
In this section, we explore versions of reverse H¨older inequalities satisfied by operator Ap
weights. This may at first seem to contradict [46, 58], where counterexamples are given to show
that the A2 condition does not imply a reverse H¨older inequality for operator-valued weights in in-
finite dimensions. The example sketched in [46, paragraph after Corollary 4.7] is very simple; [58,
Theorem 5.1] provides a more elaborate example to show that even the weighted L2 boundedness
of the Hilbert transform, which is known to be strictly stronger than the A2 condition in infinite
dimensions, still does not imply a reverse H¨older inequality. However, both [46, 58] interpret the
notion of a reverse H¨older inequality in the sense introduced in [17, Eq. (2.1)] for matrix weights;
notwithstanding its established merits in the finite-dimensional theory, we wish to argue that this
is no longer the “right” definition in infinite dimensions. The support for this argument comes,
on the one hand, from the counterexamples of [46, 58] just mentioned and, on the other hand,
from our proposal of a substitute notion and the results that we are able to obtain by this alternate
version.
Proposition 5.1. If V ∈Ap, then there are ε, η > 0 such that
ρŁp+ε(Q,V)(e) ≲ρŁp(Q,V)(e)
and

for all cubes Q ⊂Rn and all e ∈X and e∗∈X∗.

7. Stability of weights under some operations
In this section, we record two lemmas of relatively elementary nature that will be helpful in
streamlining some considerations further below. Since these results have a somewhat technical
nature and we only need them for p ∈(1, ∞), we restrict our considerations to this range for
brevity, leaving the extension to p ∈(0, 1] for the interested reader.

Part 2. Operator-weighted Lp(V) spaces
8. Averaging and sparse operators
The following result is an analogue of [55, Lemma 2.1] and [1, Lemma 2.2]. The former deals
with p ∈(1, ∞) and X = Cd, the latter with p = 2 and a general Hilbert space X. The former, like
our present study, is also written in the formalism of the norms ρ, while the latter makes use of
the explicit form of the reducing operators available for p = 2. Nevertheless, our argument below,
passing through certain quotient norms, is more closely modelled after the one in [1].

Remark 8.2. A curious detail of the proof above is that we manage to identify (Ker P)⊥, a priori
a subspace of functionals (Lp(V; X))∗, with an explicit subspace of functions Lp′(V−∗, X∗), without
the need to know whether the full spaces (Lp(V; X))∗and Lp′(V−∗, X∗) coincide. Indeed, in the
generality of arbitrary Banach spaces that we consider, this coincidence in general fails, as it
would require the so-called Radon–Nikod´ym property of X∗(see e.g. [39, Section 1.3.b]).
Corollary 8.3. Let X be a Banach space, p ∈(1, ∞), and V be an L (X)-valued p-weight such
that V−∗is a p′-weight. Then the averaging operators f 7→1Q⟨f⟩Q are bounded on Lp(V; X),
uniformly over all cubes Q ⊂Rn, if and only if V ∈Ap.

As a further corollary, we obtain the boundedness of a class of sparse operators, previously
considered by [49] in the context of A2-weighted L2 spaces of Hilbert space -valued functions, in
the more general setting of Ap-weighted Lp spaces of Banach space-valued functions. We should
note, however, that our Corollary 8.4 below is not strictly an extension of [49, Theorem 2.1], since
these authors obtain a somewhat sharper control, requiring the A2 property only for the cubes that
appear in the sparse collection. It might seem at first that this is the case in our argument as well,
but the fact that we apply the reverse H¨older inequality (Proposition 5.1) makes implicit use of the
Ap condition for the subcubes of these cubes as well.

9. Unbounded commutators
The aim of this section is to prove the following statement:
Theorem 9.1. Let X be an infinite-dimensional Banach space and let p ∈(1, ∞). Then there exists
a function B ∈BMOso(R; L (X)) with B∗∈BMOso(R; L (X∗)) such that its commutator [B, H]
with the Hilbert transform
H f(x) := lim
ε→0

is not bounded on Lp(R; X).
While not making direct reference to operator-weighted spaces, this result is closely related,
and this connection will be elaborated in the following section, where we return to the setting
of Lp(V). In the special case that p = 2 and X is a Hilbert space, Theorem 9.1 is contained in
[32, Theorem 1.2], [54, Theorem 1.2], or [34, Theorem 1.3]. Formally, the said results state a
dimensional growth of the commutator norms in finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces, but it is easy
to obtain the infinite-dimensional version from this by noting that an infinite-dimensional Hilbert
space can be written as an ℓ2 direct sum containing all the finite-dimensional versions.
To construct a function B as in Theorem 9.1, we will use two different methods depending on
the Banach space X. The first method works if X is not a UMD space [39, Definition 4.2.1], while
the second method requires X to be K-convex [39, Definition 4.3.9]. Since every UMD space is
K-convex [39, Proposition 4.3.10], the two cases cover all Banach spaces, even with some overlap,
since not every K-convex space has UMD.
9.1. Scalar commutators and the UMD property. For Banach spaces that fail the UMD prop-
erty, Theorem 9.1 will be a consequence of the following:
Proposition 9.2. Let X be an infinite-dimensional Banach space and let p ∈(1, ∞). Then the
following conditions are equivalent:
(i) X is a UMD space;
(ii) [b, H] is bounded on Lp(R; X) for all scalar-valued b ∈BMO(R);
(iii) [b, H] is bounded on Lp(R; X) for all scalar-valued b ∈L∞(R).
The statement is perhaps expected, in view of the well-known characterisation of the UMD
condition by the boundedness of the Hilbert transform on Lp(R; X). While this will be used in the
proof below, observe that some work still needs to be done, since the (un)boundedness of H is not
the same as the (un)boundedness of a commutator [b, H], and their equivalence will in any case
require some conditions on the function b. Proposition 9.2 continues the tradition of characterising
the UMD condition by the boundedness of various operators, like the Hilbert transform [8, 16],
imaginary powers (−△)is of the Laplacian [37], or other Fourier multipliers with sufficient structure
[31]. (The mentioned results can also be found in [39, Theorem 5.1.1], [40, Corollary 10.5.2], and
[41, Corollary 13.3.10], respectively.) In contrast to all these examples, the commutators [b, H]
in Proposition 9.2 are not translation-invariant operators.
Proof of Proposition 9.2. (i) ⇒(ii): This can be proved by repeating the classical Cauchy integral
trick of [19], where the scalar-valuedness of b is used, while the vector-valuedness of f poses no
problem. An easy identity followed by the Cauchy integral formula shows that

9.2. Operator commutators and K-convexity. We now turn to the more delicate part in the
proof of Theorem 9.1. While Proposition 9.2 provides us with a required function B = b for
non-UMD spaces, it also shows that a scalar-valued function will not suffice in the case of UMD
spaces, and hence a genuinely operator-valued construction will be necessary. While additional
steps will be needed, it turns out that we can make use of the examples already available in finite-

To proceed, we will need to embed the Hilbert space examples into a given Banach space X in a
quantitatively controlled manner. This will be accomplished with the help of the following result,
which depends on the notion of the K-convexity (see [39, Definition 4.3.9] or [40, Section 7.4.a])
of a Banach space.
Theorem 9.7 ([23]). Let X be a K-convex normed space. Then there are positive constants c,C
depending only on X with the following properties. Every finite-dimensional subspace F ⊂X with
dim F = n has a further subspace G ⊂F such that dimG ≥cnc and
(i) there is a Hilbert space H and an isomorphism ι : G →H with
∥ι∥∥ι−1∥≤2;
(ii) there is a projection P of X onto G with ∥P∥≤C.
Proof. While this is implicitly contained in [23], we indicate the required details to obtain the
above formulation from the results explicitly stated in [23]. First, [23, Corollary 8.3] contains a
similar statement except that the K-convexity is replaced by the assumption that both X and X∗
have some finite cotype q, q∗< ∞(see [40, Definition 7.1.1]) and conclusion (ii) is replaced by
∥P∥≤27Kn(X) for a quantity Kn(X) defined at the beginning of [23, Section 8].
Now, suppose that X is K-convex, as assumed in Theorem 9.7. Then X has some non-trivial
type p > 1 and some finite cotype q < ∞by [40, Proposition 7.4.12], and then also X∗has finite
cotype q∗= p′ < ∞by [40, Proposition 7.1.13]. Thus X satisfies the assumptions, and hence the
conclusions, of [23, Corollary 8.3]. Moreover, [23, Proposition 9.1] shows that the K-convexity
is equivalent to supn∈N Kn(X) < ∞. Thus, the conclusions of [23, Corollary 8.3] take exactly the
form stated in Theorem 9.7, with C = 27 supn∈N Kn(X) < ∞depending only on X.
□
Corollary 9.8. Let X be a K-convex normed space with dim X = ∞. Then it has subspaces G of
any given finite dimension with properties (i) and (ii).
Proof. Since we can take any n in Theorem 9.7, the said theorem guarantees that we can find G
with dimG ≥cnc larger than any given number. It remains to see that we can find G with dimG
equal to any given number m. To see this, let G ⊂X and H be the spaces provided by Theorem
9.7 with dimG = dim H > m. Let H′ ⊂H be a Hilbert space with dim H′ = m, and let Q be the
orthogonal projection of H onto H′. If ι : G →H is the isomorphism provided by Theorem 9.7,
then G′ := ι−1H′ ⊂ι−1H = G ⊂X is a subspace with dimG′ = dim H′ = m, and the restriction
ι′ := ι|G′ is an isomorphism of ι′ : G′ →H′ with
∥ι′∥∥(ι′)−1∥≤∥ι∥∥ι−1∥≤2.
Moreover, P′ := ι−1 ◦Q ◦ι ◦P : X →G′ is a projection of norm
∥P′∥≤∥ι−1∥∥Q∥∥ι∥∥P∥≤∥ι−1∥· 1 · ∥ι∥· C ≤2C.
Hence G′ ⊂X with the given dimension dimG′ = m satisfies (i) and (ii) of Theorem 9.7 with 2C
in place of C, but this is just another constant that depends only on X.
□

Proof. Clearly, the first case of (9.6) defines b on [4k, 4k + 1], and the last one on [4k + 2, 4k + 3].

j≥0 I j = (0, 1), the second case defines b on (4k−1, 4k), the third one on (4k+1, 4k+2).
As k ∈Z varies, this uniquely defines b at every point x ∈R.

Proof of Theorem 9.1. If X is non-UMD (resp. K-convex), then the required B is produced by

10. Unbounded Hilbert transforms
The main result of this section is the following theorem, which shows the insufficiency of
the Ap condition to guarantee the boundedness of the Hilbert transform on the operator-weighed
Lp(V; X) space, whenever p ∈(1, ∞) and X is an arbitrary Banach space. In the special case that
p = 2 and X is an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space, this is contained in [32, Theorem 1.3] or
[34, Theorem 1.1]. Formally, these results show the existence of a sequence of finite-dimensional
weights, whose A2 constants remain bounded while the norms of the weighted Hilbert transforms
grow without limit as the dimension increases to infinity. However, since an infinite-dimensional
Hilbert space can be written as an ℓ2 direct sum containing all the finite-dimensional versions, the
infinite-dimensional counterexample readily follows.
Theorem 10.1. Let p ∈(1, ∞) and X be an arbitrary Banach space. Then there exists V ∈
Ap(R; L (X)) such that the Hilbert transform is unbounded on Lp(V; X).
As in Theorem 9.1, we will consider two cases for the Banach space X. However, this time
the case that X is not UMD is entirely trivial: in this case, the Hilbert transform is unbounded on
Lp(R; X), and hence we can simply take V ≡I, the identity operator on X.
In the case that X is K-convex, the proof of Theorem 10.1 will make use of the constructions
in the proof of Theorem 9.1 through a close link of the two types of (un)boundedness results that
goes back to [33], and will be reviewed shortly. However, while the essence of this link remains in
general Banach spaces, there is a devil in the details, which prevents a soft deduction of Theorem
10.1 from Theorem 9.1 that one can do in a Hilbert space (and more generally, but not always),
and seems to necessitate revisiting also several details of the proof.
The following lemma gives the basic construction of Ap weights from BMO functions. The
case that p = 2 and X1 = X2 is a Hilbert space goes back to [33, Lemma 2.1]. To streamline the

Remark 10.3. Before proceeding with the proof of Theorem 10.1 for a general K-convex Ba-
nach space X, let us indicate an easier argument under an additional assumption that the Ba-
nach space X is decomposable, meaning that it admits a representation X = X1 ⊕X2 as a di-
rect sum of two infinite-dimensional Banach spaces {X j}2
j=1.
Let B ∈BMOso(R; L (X1, X2))
with B∗∈BMOso(R; L (X∗
2, X∗
1)) be a function, provided by Proposition 9.12, such that [B, H]
is not bounded from Lp(R; X1) to Lp(R; X2). Let V : R →L (X) be defined by (10.1); thus
V ∈Ap(R; L (X)) by Lemma 10.2.


## Page 37

37
Note that H is bounded on Lp(V; X) if and only if VHV−1 is bounded on Lp(R; X). Relative to
the direct sum splitting X = X1 ⊕X2 and the induced splitting Lp(R; X) = Lp(R; X1) ⊕Lp(R; X2),
this latter operator is given by
VHV−1 =

Thus, a necessary condition for the boundedness of H : Lp(V; X) →Lp(V; X) is the boundedness
of [B, H] : Lp(R; X1) →Lp(R; X2), which was specifically excluded by Proposition 9.12. Hence,
the proof of Theorem 10.1 can be completed in this soft way in the special case of decomposable
Banach spaces.
While this property holds for most classical Banach spaces, it does not hold in general. In-
deed, there even exist hereditarily indecomposable (H.I.) Banach spaces X, meaning that not only
X itself but all of its infinite-dimensional closed subspaces are indecomposable (i.e., not decom-
posable). The first such example is due to Gowers and Maurey [36], and others have been found
since then. Notably, Ferenczi [22] has constructed an H.I. space X that is uniformly convex and
hence in particular K-convex. (See the end of this Remark for this implication.) Thus, the above
construction of V is not possible in arbitrary K-convex spaces, and not even in the smaller class of
uniformly convex spaces.
To complete the proof of Theorem 10.1, we would actually only need this construction in the
still smaller class of UMD spaces. Whether every UMD space is decomposable appears to be
open. If this turns out to be the case, then a simpler proof of Theorem 10.1 will be available,
and we completed it already. However, in the lack of knowing such a result, we adopt a different
approach below, which does not require decomposability.
The idea is the following: Instead of using the finite-dimensional functions Bk to build an
infinite-dimensional B and then using this to build the weight V by Lemma 10.2 (as we did
above, assuming decomposability), we use each finite-dimensional function Bk to build a finite-
dimensional weight Vk by Lemma 10.2, and then we use the finite-dimensional weights Vk to build
an infinite-dimensional weight V in a similar way as the functions Bk were used to build B. This
turns out to work, as we will see below.
[The implication from uniform convexity to K-convexity, which we mentioned above, is well
known, but seems difficult to quote as a single result. It can be obtained, e.g., from the following
chain: if X is uniformly convex, then X∗is uniformly smooth (by [57, Proposition 10.21]), thus
X∗admits an equivalent p-smooth norm for some p > 1 (see [57, Definition 10.18 and 10.30,
and Theorem 10.25]), and therefore X∗has type p > 1 (by [57, Proposition 10.39]); hence X∗is
K-convex (see [40, Theorem 7.4.23]) and thus so is X (by [40, Proposition 7.4.5]).]
Having explained in Remark 10.3 why a soft construction of the weight is not available in
general, we now turn to the details of the alternative approach that was also sketched above.
Proposition 10.4. Let X be an infinite-dimensional K-convex Banach space. Then there exists
V ∈Ap(R; L (X)) such that the Hilbert transform is not bounded on Lp(V; X).
Proof. The construction of V involves a variation of the construction of B in Proposition 9.12. By

Theorem 9.7, and dimGk = 2Nk can be taken even and as large as we like. Let ιk, Pk, Hk, and
C have the same meaning as in Theorem 9.7 with Gk in place of G; note that C depends only on
X, and is hence independent of k. Since dim Hk = dimGk = 2N, we can write it as a direct sum
Hk = Ek ⊗Ek of two copies of the same Nk-dimensional Hilbert space Ek.

Proof of Theorem 10.1. If X is not a UMD space, the Hilbert transform is already unbounded on
Lp(R; X), so it suffices to take V ≡I, the identity operator. If X is K-convex, then the required
weight is provided by Proposition 10.4. Since every UMD space is K-convex (see [39, Proposition
4.3.10]), these two cases cover all Banach spaces.
□

Part 3. Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces
12. Main definitions
In this part, we develop the real-variable theory of Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces associ-
ated with the Ap weights. We begin with the following definition of Littlewood–Paley functions.
Definition 12.1. Let
√
2 < α < β < π. We say that φ ∈S (Rn) is an (α, β)-Littlewood–Paley
function if
1[ 1

For a Littlewood–Paley function φ and j ∈Z, we denote

Most of the time, we could simply consider Littlewood–Paley functions with some fixed (α, β),

Remark 12.2. Whenever φ exists a Littlewood–Paley function, there exists another Littlewood–
Paley function χ such that χ j ∗φj = φj. Indeed, if φ is an (α, β)-Littlewood–Paley function, it

13. Fourier analysis preliminaries
In this section, we establish some basic tools in Fourier analysis, including the Calder´on re-
producing formula and the sampling type inequality in infinite-dimensional target spaces. They
will play key roles in developing the real-variable theory of operator-valued Besov and Triebel–
Lizorkin spaces.

We now turn to the sampling type inequality. Recall that a seminorm is like a norm except
that it is allowed to have a nontrivial null space. Moreover, we need the following concept of
u-seminorms.
Definition 13.2. Let u ∈(0, 1]. A u-(semi)norm ρ is like a (semi)norm except that the triangle
inequality is replaced by
ρ(e + v)u ≤ρ(e)u + ρ(v)u
∀e, v ∈X.
It easily follows that any u-(semi)norm is also an s-(semi)norm for all s ∈(0, u). In particular,
any norm is also a u-norm for all u ∈(0, 1). Based on this, we now derive the following sampling
type inequality.

The proof of Theorem 14.3 is now a matter of collecting the pieces:
Proof of Theorem 14.3. The “≳” part is simply the Besov case of Proposition 14.6.
To prove the “≲” part of the present theorem, we only need to check all the assumptions of
Proposition 14.4 with {ρQ}Q∈D therein replaced by {ρŁr(Q,V)}Q∈D.
Noting that the assumptions of Proposition 14.6 are the same as those of Theorem 14.3, we can
use (14.7), which implies both (14.2) and, in combination with Lemma 6.7, also (14.3).
Thus the assumptions, and hence the conclusions, of Proposition 14.4 are satisfied, which com-
pletes the proof of the “≲” part holds.
□
14.2. Triebel–Lizorkin spaces. In this subsection, we extend Theorem 14.3 to Triebel–Lizorkin
spaces. We still cannot do this for whole range q ∈(0, ∞]. Indeed, for V ∈Aw with w > 0, we
need the following optimal reverse H¨older lifting index εV:
εV := sup ε ∈(0, ∞) : ρŁw+ε(Q,V)(e) ≲ρŁw(Q,V)(e)
holds for all cubes Q ⊂Rn and all e ∈X	.
Note that, by Proposition 5.1, the assumption that V ∈Aw always guarantees εV > 0. Then we
have the following equivalences for Triebel–Lizorkin spaces.
Theorem 14.7. Let p, r ∈(0, ∞), V ∈Amax{p,r} with optimal reverse H¨older lifting index εV > 0,
and s ∈R. If q ∈(0, max{p, r} + εV), then, for any f ∈S ′
∞(Rn; X),
∥f∥˙Fsp,q(V) ≈∥f∥˙Fsp,q(ρŁr(D,V))
(14.8)
and, for any sequence t = {tQ}Q∈D of vectors in X,
∥t∥˙f sp,q(V) ≈∥t∥˙f sp,q(ρŁr(D,V)),
(14.9)
where the positive equivalence constants are independent of f and t.
Remark 14.8. If, in Theorem 14.7, let p = r and X = Cm, then the equivalences (14.8) and (14.9)
hold for all q ∈(0, ∞], which were proved in [28, Theorem 3.1]. However, when X is infinite-
dimensional, we only show these under the restriction q ∈(0, r + εV) in Theorem 14.7. When
q = ∞, we prove that the equivalence (14.9) may not be true for general infinite-dimensional
case in Proposition 14.13. It is still unclear whether Theorem 14.7 holds for the remaining case
q ∈[r + εV, ∞).
We first consider the “≳” part of Theorem 14.7. Indeed, this has already been done for (14.8)
in Proposition 14.6. Hence, we now deal with the “≳” part of (14.9) as follows.

16. Relation of Triebel–Lizorkin spaces to Lp(V)
In the classical theory of function spaces, one of the key features of Triebel–Lizorkin spaces
is that they contain the usual Lp spaces as the special case ˙F0
p,2(Rn) = Lp(Rn) for p ∈(1, ∞).
Under the Ap condition, this remains true even for matrix-weighted spaces, as shown in slightly
different versions in [28, 42, 66]. However, this cannot possibly extend to the level of generality
that we consider: By Corollary 15.15, the Hilbert transform is bounded on ˙F0
p,q(ρŁp(D,V)) for all
p ∈(0, ∞) and q ∈(0, ∞], and on ˙F0
p,q(V) for all 0 < q ≤p < ∞, whenever V ∈Ap. On the
other hand, Theorem 10.1 shows that this condition is insufficient for the boundedness on Lp(V).
Thus V ∈Ap does not guarantee the coincidence of Lp(V) with any of the Triebel–Lizorkin spaces
˙F0
p,q(ρŁp(D,V)) or ˙F0
p,q(V) with q in the indicated ranges.
On the other hand, for functions taking values in a Banach space, even in the unweighted case,
the best that one can say in general is the “sandwich” result
˙F0
p,1(Rn; X) ⊂Lp(Rn; X) ⊂˙F0
p,∞(Rn; X),

17. Almost diagonal operators
In this section, we study the boundedness of the following class of almost diagonal operators
on Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces. This is a cornerstone for developing further properties of
these spaces in the subsequent sections.
Definition 17.1. A matrix B = {bQR}Q,R∈D is said to be (D, E, F)-almost diagonal if
|bQR| ≲B−E,−F,−D(Q, R)
where the right-hand side is defined in (6.6).
Remark 17.2. Definition 17.1 is equivalent to [11, Definition 4.1], although it was there formulated
slightly differently. The said [11, Definition 4.1] seems to be the first place where this condition
was formulated with generic parameters D, E, F ∈R. With specific choices of parameters, the
definition goes back to [25, p. 53], and reappears in [59, Definition 8.1] and [28, Definition 2.5],
each time with different specific parameters for the particular context.
Before proceeding to our main results, we first recall the classical result. Recall that the scalar

Remark 17.4. As showed in [11, Sections 7 and 9], the assumption (17.1) on (D, E, F) is optimal.
More precisely, if a = b or a = f and q ≥min{1, p}, and if every (D, E, F)-almost diagonal
operator B is bounded on ˙as
p,q, then, by [11, Sections 7 and 9], the triple (D, E, F) satisfies (17.1).
In this section, we deal with the boundedness of B on average and pointwise-weighted spaces
separately. First, in Subsection 17.1, we establish the boundedness of almost diagonal operators
on average-weighted spaces ˙As

optimal assumption on (D, E, F) is still unknown (see Remark 17.7). Next, in Subsection 17.2, we
improve the assumption on (D, E, F) for pointwise-weighted spaces ˙As

and we prove that this assumption is optimal in some sense (see Remark 17.13).

17.3. Sharpness of the assumptions. We now claim that the assumptions on (D, E, F) in The-
orem 17.12 are optimal in some sense. Note that the key to show Theorem 17.12 is (17.11).
Motivated by Example 11.1, we now prove that the estimate (17.11) cannot be improved in the
infinite-dimensional case and hence, in this sense, the assumptions in Theorem 17.12 are optimal.

Part 4. Abstract discretizable spaces
18. An axiomatic framework and examples
In this remaining part of this work, we extend various further results from the theory of (un-
weighted, weighted, or matrix-weighted) Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces to the operator-
weighted spaces that we have considered. Having obtained the φ-transform characterization and
the boundedness of almost diagonal operators on a given function space, many further real-variable
properties can be established in an axiomatic way. For this purpose, we introduce the following
framework.
Definition 18.1. Let d, e, and f be three real numbers.
A quasi-normed space ˙A(Rn; X) ⊂
S ′
∞(Rn; X) is called a homogeneous (d, e, f)-discretizable space if the following three conditions
hold:
(i) S∞⊗X ⊂˙A(Rn; X), where S∞⊗X is as in (15.7).
(ii) There exists a quasi-norm space ˙a(Rn; X) consisting of sequences t = {tQ}Q∈D, where each
tQ ∈X, such that, for any Littlewood–Paley functions φ, ψ that satisfy (15.3),
S φ : ˙A(Rn; X) →˙a(Rn; X)
and
Tψ : ˙a(Rn; X) →˙A(Rn; X)
are bounded and Tψ ◦S φ acts as the identity on ˙A(Rn; X).
(iii) For any triple (D, E, F) that satisfies D > d, E > e, and F > f and for any (D, E, F)-almost
diagonal operator B, the series P
R∈D bQRtR is absolutely convergent in X for all Q ∈D and
all sequences t = {tR}R∈D ∈˙a(Rn; X), and B is bounded on ˙a(Rn; X).

In the next Section 19, we will prove a characterization of (d, e, f)-discretizable spaces in terms
of so-called molecules. We already introduce the relevant concepts here, to show how the various
parameters of molecules and discretizable spaces interact with each other.

We introduce the concept of molecules as follows; see, for instance, [12, Definition 3.4]. Since
we are dealing with space of X-valued distributions, it might appear natural to also make the
molecules X-valued. However, we will only need scalar-valued molecules; with X-valued coeffi-
cients, they will serve as building blocks of X-valued distributions in the molecular characterization
further below.

19. Molecular characterization
The goal of this section is to establish the molecular characterization of operator-weighted
Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces within the abstract framework of (d, e, f)-discretizable spaces
from Definition 18.1. Before we state the molecular characterization, we need to show that certain
dual pairings ⟨f, Φ⟩are well defined.
Proposition 19.1. Let ˙A(Rn; X) be a homogeneous (d, e, f)-discretizable space with some real
parameters (d, e, f). If f ∈˙A(Rn; X) and Φ is an ˙a-analysis molecule on P ∈D, then, for any
Littlewood–Paley functions φ, ψ that satisfy (15.3), the expression

choices of φ and ψ.

20. Wavelet characterization
In this section, we first establish the wavelet characterization of the abstract space ˙A(Rn; X)
and, as an application, we give the wavelet characterization of Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces
introduced in Definition 12.3.

Proof of Proposition 20.1. We first consider (i). By Definitions 18.4 and 18.3, ˙a-analysis and ˙a-
synthesis molecules are (K, L, M, N)-molecules with sufficiently large values of the parameters, as
specified in Definition 18.4.
From φ, ψ ∈CN
c (R) and the way that Ψ(λ)
Q is formed by scaling and translation, the molecular
size condition (Definition 18.3(i)) holds with K as large as we want, and the molecular smoothness
condition (Definition 18.3(iii) holds with the same N as the order of the wavelets and arbitrarily
large M. Hence, both K and M (trivially) and N (by assumption (20.2)) are sufficiently large for
both types of molecules.
For λ ∈Λn, assumption (20.1) shows that the molecular cancellation condition (Definition
18.3(ii)) also holds with L = N, which is sufficiently large for analysis/synthesis molecules (Defi-
nition 18.4) by assumption (20.2).
For (ii), under the additional assumption f −n
2 < 0, Remark 18.6 shows that we can take L < 0,
meaning that no cancellation is needed for synthesis molecules. (This is needed for λ′ = 0, in
which case there is no cancellation.) The fact that Ψ(λ)
Q satisfies the other molecular conditions
(size and smoothness) with relevant K, M, N is the same as in case (i).
Finally, we deal with (iii). By part (i), we know that Ψω = Ψ(λ)
Q (with λ ∈Λn) is an ˙a-analysis
molecule. Then the claim of (iii) is a direct application of Proposition 19.1 with Φ = Ψω.
□

21. Calder´on–Zygmund operators
In this section, we study the boundedness of Calder´on–Zygmund operators and the related T(1)
theorem. First, we give a general extension criterion of a given operator T ∈L(S , S ′) in Sub-
section 21.1. Then we establish the T(1) theorem of Calder´on–Zygmund operators in Subsection
21.2. Finally, in Subsection 21.3, we present some concrete examples of Calder´on–Zygmund
operators and corresponding T(1) theorems.

distributions.

21.2. T(1) theorem for Calder´on–Zygmund operators. We now turn to the actual discussion of
Calder´on–Zygmund operators. The following classical definitions are standard. Let D := C∞
c with
the usual inductive limit topology. We denote by D′ the space of all continuous linear functionals
on D, equipped with the weak-∗topology. If T ∈L(S , S ′), then, by the well-known Schwartz
kernel theorem, we find that there exists K ∈S ′(Rn × Rn) such that ⟨Tφ, ψ⟩= ⟨K, φ ⊗ψ⟩for all
φ, ψ ∈S . This K is called the Schwartz kernel of T.
The adjoint operator T ∗∈L(S , S ′) of T is defined by setting ⟨Tφ, ψ⟩= ⟨T ∗ψ, φ⟩for all
φ, ψ ∈S . Then, from the definition, we easily infer that, if K ∈S ′(Rn × Rn) is the Schwartz
kernel of T, then the Schwartz kernel K∗of T ∗satisfies ⟨K, φ ⊗ψ⟩= ⟨K∗, ψ ⊗φ⟩for all φ, ψ ∈S .
Inspired by [29, 64], to establish the boundedness of Calder´on–Zygmund operators on Besov–
Triebel–Lizorkin spaces, we use the following kernel estimates from [12, Definition 6.11].

Remark 21.4. Let T ∈L(S , S ′). We have the following observations.
(i) If F ≤0 < E, then the conditions (21.8) and (21.9) are both void and hence, for any σ ∈
{0, 1}, CZKσ(E; F) = CZK0(E; 0). Hence, the set CZK0(E; 0) means the set of operators
with Calder´on–Zygmund estimates of order E in the x variable, with no assumptions on
the y variable. Furthermore, if E ∈(0, 1], then the operator T ∈CZK0(E; 0) satisfying
T ∗∈CZO0(F; 0) is precisely the classical Calder´on–Zygmund operator presented in [21,
Definition 5.11].
(ii) For more examples of operators in CZKσ(E; F), we refer to [12, Remark 6.13].

In [28, 59], the authors first present results for general Calder´on–Zygmund operators in the
spirit of Corollary 21.10, and then give a separate treatment for convolution-type operators. How-
ever, the need for this latter seems to come from a slight misunderstanding in [59, Remark 9.12]:
“Note that the condition T ∗(yγ) = 0, |γ| ≤N, can be very restrictive; for example, the Hilbert
transform does not satisfy this condition for |γ| > 0.” This is repeated in [28, pp. 526–527].
However, if one interprets T ∗(xγ) in the usual way as a distribution modulo polynomials of
order |γ|, then we do have T ∗(xγ) = 0 for all multi-indices γ for the Hilbert, the Riesz, and other
classical convolution transforms with sufficiently regular kernels. Indeed, by [64, Proposition
2.2.17], every L-smooth Calder´on–Zygmund operator T satisfies T(xγ) = 0 (resp. T ∗(xθ) = 0) as
a distribution on D|γ| (resp. D|θ|), where

This fact seems to have been partially overlooked in [59, 28]. Instead of needing a separate

Corollary 21.13. Assume L ∈N and T is an L-smooth Calder´on–Zygmund operator. Then T ∈

21.6. This then finishes the proof of Corollary 21.13.

22. Trace and extension operators
In this section, we study the trace and the extension operators. Due to the specific structure of
these two classes of operators, it seems difficult to obtain satisfactory results within the general
framework ˙A(Rn; X). Hence, we focus on the pointwise and the average-weighted spaces in this
section directly.

Since w ∈[p, ∞) by assumption, it follows that min{p, ud} = min{p, 1, w} = min{p, 1}. Thus
assumption (22.23) of the corollary coincides with assumption (22.17) of Theorem 22.5 for the
special case of the quasi-norms now under consideration. Similarly, the assumption (20.7) on
N is the special case, for these parameters, of assumption (20.6) made in Theorem 22.5. Thus
Corollary 22.6 follows by specialising Theorem 22.5 to this case and using the equivalence of the
pointwise-weighted and average-weighted spaces already mentioned.
□
